**Quarter synthesis:** with the backlog ($638B RPO) already taken as won by most of the sell-side, the debate has shifted from demand to financing — the funding gap, dilution via the ATM and off-balance-sheet leases are the new battleground (JPM/MS bears vs Bernstein/DB bulls); the optical OCI "miss" has been reframed as timing, not demand. **07-11: Mizuho (Rakesh) adds a new bull PT ($320, Outperform), naming ORCL a key neocloud/OCI capex driver inside a sector call for neocloud/Tier-II CSP capex of $398B'27/$485B'28 (share 22%→35% by '28) — a demand-side counterweight to the financing bears.** **07-20: the bears finally get a rating — Rothschild & Co Redburn (Haissl/Han) initiates the page's FIRST SELL, cutting its PT to $110 from $155 on a "Margin Call" thesis (group GM -510bp; the consensus mid-30s OCI GM recovery leans on a database bet growing only 29% YoY vs the 50%+ CAGR implied; RDBN EPS well below cons). BofA (Post) piles on the balance-sheet/dilution bear (12% RPO conversion vs MSFT 25%, ~$20bn equity still needed, capex-plateau-vs-1GW→10GW disconnect). The lone offset is on the credit axis — JPM UPGRADES ORCL credit to overweight (debt largely done for CY26), even as the ~$20bn equity overhang remains the swing factor. Net: the financing + database-led-margin-recovery debate is now the whole story.** **07-02/06/21 — a third, previously-unlogged axis arrives from SemiAnalysis, and it is about PRICE, not demand or financing.** The 07-02 "Meta Compute" note lands the sharpest independent negative on the page: SpaceX's Anthropic/Google deals priced at **3x/4x peers' revenue per MW** (on a roughly identical cost structure, so an even wider profit/MW gap), structured 3-year with a 90-day bilateral cancel; neoclouds are financing-excluded from that segment and the top-3 hyperscalers passed for higher-value long-term options, leaving "**only two companies that can truly capitalize… Oracle and Meta**" — and for Oracle "**a massive blow… yet another piece of evidence that they could've done a much better job at monetizing all of their gigawatts of compute**," with the Oracle-vs-SpaceX valuation divergence as the exhibit. Note this is orthogonal to the bear consensus on the page (financing/dilution) and to the bull consensus (backlog): it concedes the gigawatts and attacks the realised price on them. The partial rebuttal is management's own: Oracle prices for an **8-10-year relationship** and refuses to overcharge into a renewal problem (WFC 06-11) — i.e. the same fact viewed at opposite contract durations, and the reason BYOH/prepaid (GM ~55% vs ~40% comparable) matters more than headline $/MW. Two offsets from the same publisher: 07-06 names ORCL the **first hyperscaler to fund with debt** but sizes that debt market at **~$7.1T by 2029** with landlords structurally preferring 10-15-yr hyperscaler offtakes over neoclouds (whose colo yield-on-cost runs 3-5% higher) — financing-gap-supportive, not gap-denying; and 07-21 gives Oracle a rare infra compliment ("building large, functional networks out of simpler hardware without trying to reinvent it"). Net-net: the build is disciplined, the financing is fundable, and the open question is now whether Oracle's realised revenue per MW is a deliberate duration trade or a franchise-level under-monetisation.** **07-26 (read-through, UBS MSFT pre-print note) — the outside view of Oracle is now explicitly a cautionary tale, and it adds a cause the page had not isolated.** UBS uses ORCL three times as the negative precedent: "the Street is not rewarding the stocks of AI infra providers that signal higher capex spend and/or don't post upside to cloud revs growth and margins (**see Oracle and Google**)," "capex raises from both were **not greeted with applause**," and "**the Google and Oracle equity transactions** have raised questions as to whether Microsoft may be forced to raise external capital." The genuinely new attribution is the *timing and cause* of the de-rating: UBS dates the weakness in "Microsoft, Oracle, CoreWeave and other AI infra providers" to **early June** and pins it on **open-model share gains against the frontier labs**, grouping ORCL with MSFT as "the hyperscalers with large frontier lab exposure" — a cause orthogonal to the financing/dilution bear this page has been running, and one that sits closer to the 06-23 UBS token-optimization read already on file than to the Redburn margin short. **And on that margin short, UBS is now the other side:** "we've made the same observation about the margin performance at AWS, Oracle and others — **on the surface, AI capex/revs are not landing at the abysmal margin levels that the bears feared**." That is a Buy-rated house ($285) directly contesting Redburn's SELL/$110 premise, and it is left on the page as an open disagreement. Valuation context from the same note's peer table (7/24/26): **ORCL at 15.4x/12.6x CY26E/CY27E non-GAAP P/E on +25.9%/+38.7% revenue growth — the fastest CY27E grower in the twelve-name comp set, at a discount to the 16.1x/14.4x mean** — which is the dispersion on this page reduced to two numbers. **RE-EVALUATED 2026-08-12 (/run-inbox, hyperscaler cluster — 1 read-through source): a new structural bear that is NOT about funding, and it deserves to sit alongside the funding debate rather than inside it. Jefferies (08-10) documents gigawatt monetisation moving from $8-12bn to $30-50bn in six months — a 3-5x repricing of compute — and then points out that Oracle and [[CRWV]], alone among the compute sellers, CANNOT PARTICIPATE, because five-year locked-in unit economics are the precondition for the debt raise that funds the build. On this reading the RPO is not only the asset the bulls describe; it is also the instrument that fixes Oracle's realised revenue-per-gigawatt near the OLD price level while the hyperscalers reprice on-demand and spot. Two corollaries worth carrying: (i) the labs are not signing five-year paper at $30-50bn/GW — the deals at those prices carry 90-day cancellation — so the premium and the duration are mutually exclusive; (ii) Oracle's [[OPENAI]] relationship is "very much bare metal," where [[MSFT]] monetises the same customer across Cosmos, Blob and application services, which is the attach gap the margin debate keeps circling. ⚠️ Read-through only — no ORCL rating, PT or estimate attached.** **RE-EVALUATED 2026-08-13 (/run-inbox — the ORCL FUNDING CLUSTER: UBS 08-05, DB 08-07 and JPM 08-13 read in full for the first time). Four things change on this page, and none of them is "the backlog is fake."** **(1) THE SELL-SIDE IS QUIETLY DE-RATING WHILE PUBLICLY REITERATING.** UBS cut its PT **$285 → $245** on 08-05 (multiple 28x C27e → **19x C28e**) with estimates *unchanged*; JPM went **$210 Dec-26 → $200 Dec-27** on 08-13 while *raising* FY28 EPS to $11.10 from $10.59. Two positive-rated houses, two multiple cuts, both partially disguised by rolling the valuation year forward. **The ratings did not move; the discount rate did.** The published PT range on this name is now Bernstein $325 / Mizuho $320 / DB $300 / **UBS $245** / **JPM $200 (Dec-27)** / Redburn $110 — a ~3x spread between the highest and lowest target on a $153 stock, which is itself the story. **(2) THE FUNDING BEAR HAS BEEN BOUNDED AND DATED, AND IT IS NARROWER THAN THIS PAGE IMPLIED.** All three notes locate the gap in **FY27-FY28** and all three sit **above the Street on FY28 capex** (JPM ~$120bn, DB ~$120bn, UBS $109.7bn vs a ~$92bn consensus) — yet UBS models it away entirely by FY30 *conditional on BYOH/prepay continuing*: "highly likely that there will be no cash funding gap over the full FY27-FY30 period… [but] there most certainly IS a funding gap for FY27 and FY28." ➜ **The correct formulation of the bear is no longer "Oracle cannot fund the backlog." It is "Oracle needs roughly $20bn/yr for two years, the equity share of it is undisclosed, and BYOH/prepay — a customer concession Oracle does not control — is the swing factor."** The checkpoints are now concrete and dated: the **ATM size and mix disclosed at F1Q**, and S&P's named trigger of **FCF-positive by FY29** (exactly where UBS's model turns). **(3) THE MARGIN DEBATE MOVED IN THE BEARS' FAVOUR — OUT OF THE BULLS' OWN NOTES.** UBS published a **10% fully-loaded ROIC** (vs management's "high 20s," the entire difference being the depreciation add-back) and JPM's Risks section concedes AI-cloud gross margins sit at **"reported mid-teens"** against the 30-40% lifetime target. Those are the two most damaging margin datapoints of the quarter and both come from Buy/Overweight houses. **The bull case is now explicitly a CONVERGENCE claim, not a level claim** — which makes Redburn's SELL (07-20) better-supported than it was, and makes the 07-26 UBS line about margins "not landing at the abysmal levels that the bears feared" harder to hold on ORCL specifically than on [[MSFT]]/AWS. **(4) THE PRICE-PER-GIGAWATT ARGUMENT IS NOW ON BOTH SIDES OF THE LEDGER.** UBS independently reproduces the very gap the bears use — "$10 billion of revs per GW… investors are aware that [[SPCX]]… referenced signing deals at $30-50 billion of revs per GW" — but reads it as a **"strong upward bias to the revs per GW assumption used by Oracle investors,"** i.e. unpriced optionality as Oracle moves beyond selling GPU access. SemiAnalysis (07-02) and Jefferies (08-10) read the identical gap as a structural handicap imposed by the contract duration the debt raise requires. JPM supplies the mechanism by which UBS could be right — Oracle is "one of the only GPU-scale clouds that also owns the enterprise system of record," so bare metal is "the WEDGE" for database/PaaS attach — and even names the tactical lever: "contracting capacity **closer to delivery** of the data center." ⚠️ **Left unresolved deliberately: this is the highest-value open disagreement on the name, and it gets settled by realised revenue per GW on the 2027 cohort, not by argument.** **One number to carry into the F1Q print: [[OPENAI]] as a share of the $638bn RPO is estimated at ~40% by UBS (~$250bn, 08-05) and ~50%+ by JPM (08-13) — a ~$65bn spread on a figure Oracle has never disclosed, which alone determines how much of the backlog is a single counterparty. Press IR on it.** **🔴 08-14/08-20 — THE CREDIT DESK JUST GAVE THE FINANCING BEAR ITS HARDEST FORM, AND IT IS A DIFFERENT ARGUMENT FROM THE ONE THIS PAGE HAS BEEN HAVING.** The three August equity/funding notes (UBS 08-05, DB 08-07, JPM 08-13) all treated the FY27 mix as a *choice* — 50:50, or all-debt vs all-equity at $150/$100. **Morgan Stanley's credit team says both legs are impaired at once: *"bond yields approaching 8% and the stock down ~22% YTD, reducing the attractiveness of future equity raises as a source of funding,"* with the desk *"cautious on Oracle given its forward financing needs"* (08-14).** ➜ **That reframes the debate from "how big is the gap" to "at what price can either channel be tapped," and it is the first time a house has argued the debt and equity constraints bind simultaneously rather than substituting for each other.** The tracker six days later supplies the corroborating hard fact: **Oracle's ENTIRE $25bn of YTD issuance was a single February deal (+223% vs the prior-year period) — no public dollar issuance since February, therefore no recent pricing reference** — inside a market that has NOT slowed ($54bn July, $41bn August MTD, ~$445bn YTD vs $217bn for all of 2025) and that MS expects to keep widening through *"a busy fall."* **🗓️ MS explicitly flags ORCL and [[AVGO]] as *"front and center ahead of their respective September [reporting dates]"* — so Sep 10 is now a credit event as well as an equity one, and the ATM disclosure lands into the widening.** Two structural notes, both two-sided: **MS favours COLLATERAL-BACKED risk, which is the axis on which Oracle screens worst (the offtake *is* the collateral, and $260bn of leases sit off balance sheet); and only [[GOOG]] and [[AMZN]] have used non-dollar markets this year — a channel Oracle has not opened, which matters if the dollar market stays expensive.** ⚠️ **Guard-rails recorded so this does not get mis-aggregated later: (i) the ~$1.4trn 2027 capex figure is an AGGREGATE across AMZN/GOOG/META/MSFT/ORCL/SPCX and NO per-name split is taken from that exhibit (column-interleaved chart, unreadable from the text layer); (ii) GS Credit's "$194bn issued by the hyperscalers as of July 25" (07-27; five names incl. Oracle, ex-SPV data centres) is NOT comparable to MS's $132bn HQ-hyperscaler + $25bn Oracle split without restating — different perimeters, and neither corrects the other; (iii) the -41% YTD (UBS, 07-24) and ~-22% YTD (MS, 08-14) marks reconcile on the same ~$195 start via the rally to ~$153 — a dated difference, not a conflict.** **Separately and unrelated to financing, one supply-side identification: an Asia hardware call names ORACLE and [[META]] as the two major [[AMD]] Helios projects, with rack-level mass production ~1Q27 and rack shipment 1H27 — the delivery schedule underneath the 50,000-GPU MI450 deployment JPM cites, i.e. the AMD leg of chip-neutrality is a 1H27 rack event.** ⚠️ **That source is a severe ASR defect (13,071 `[silence]` lines around ~20KB of content, heavy homophone garbling) — the dates are usable, the names are not load-bearing, and Oracle has confirmed none of it.** **🆕🔴 UPDATED 2026-08-21 (1 source: Barclays FICC/Credit Research, "AI Infrastructure Financing — August 2026 Update", 08-20; CREDIT desk, no rating/PT/estimate — NOTHING ON THIS PAGE SUPERSEDED). The financing battleground this synthesis describes has, for the first time, been settled with PRICES rather than models — and on every observable measure Oracle is the expensive name. Four things now sit on the page as market facts rather than forecasts. ① Oracle's entire 2026 issuance is $25.0bn USD and $0 foreign from one February deal — double-sourced now (MS 08-20, Barclays 08-20) — against AMZN $92.4bn and GOOG $80.3bn, with MSFT at zero; Oracle alone among the large borrowers has no non-dollar channel. ② MS credit's "yields approaching 8%" is precise: 7.64% / 7.75% / 7.90% on the '46/'56/'66, cash prices 87-89, and +63 to +74bp of issue-to-current widening on everything 2036 and longer — against +40bp for META, +57bp SPCX, +27bp GOOGL, +9bp AMZN. Oracle started the widest and widened the most. ③ NET-NEW AND THE MOST IMPORTANT ADDITION: the off-balance-sheet channel is no longer only a lease footnote — three named third-party bonds are outstanding against an Oracle lease (RDMICH $14.0bn/974MW at 6.99%/+326, PFORGE $2.15bn/200MW at 7.36%/+295, YNDRDC $715mn/48MW at 8.67%/+426), $16.87bn / 1,222MW in total by our sum of Barclays' rows, i.e. ~67% of Oracle's own issuance raised on Oracle's covenant by other people's SPVs, with RDMICH the second-largest data-centre bond in the market behind Meta's $27.3bn Blue Owl JV. ④ And the offtake-credit gap is priced: Oracle-offtake IG at +326 against Meta-offtake at +200/+223 and Microsoft-offtake at +232/+254 — heavily confounded by tenor/structure/sponsor so the magnitude is not like-for-like, but the direction matches the ratings row exactly. ➜ WHAT THIS DOES TO THE SYNTHESIS: it does not change the shape of the debate, it removes the ambiguity from one side of it. The bull case that the funding gap will be met by a market that scales to it is CORROBORATED (data-centre bonds $35.9bn full-year 2025 → $76.2bn YTD 2026; outstandings $27.3bn → $112.1bn; ~4.0GW of near-term HY pipeline; "most developers… now repeat issuers"). The bear case that Oracle pays materially more than its peers for the same gigawatts is now MEASURED rather than argued. Both are true, and the two together are the actual state of the funding debate going into the Sep-10 print — which Barclays' complex-level crowding numbers make harder rather than easier: hyperscalers are 14% of ALL USD IG corporate supply (~17% incl. data-centre bonds), Tech is 37% of IG non-financial supply ("a record for a single sector" if sustained), and Barclays expects that supply to KEEP GROWING.** ⚠️ **Guard-rail carried forward and extended: Barclays' ~$338bn-YTD aggregate (USD-only, IG+HY+securitised+visible HPC hardware, vs $179bn full-year 2025; headline rounds to ~$340bn) is a THIRD perimeter alongside MS's $445bn/$217bn and GS's $194bn. Three definitions, none a correction of another, restate before comparing.**

**Quarter synthesis — UPDATED 2026-08-25 (2 Tier-2 backfill sources: Damnang / “The Optical Edge”, “An Aschenbrenner-Style AI Infrastructure Investment Strategy for 2028”, 2026-05-05; Citi “Inference Ahead — Open v. Closed”, 2026-06-19. NOTHING SUPERSEDED — Tier-2 material never displaces a current number, and no ORCL rating, PT, RPO, capex or margin figure on this page was overwritten).** The debate above is unchanged; two things were added underneath it. **First, the RPO ramp now has its interim step on the page — $138B → $523B (Q2 FY26) → $638B (Q4 FY26) — which matters because most of the “is the backlog real?” argument has been conducted on the endpoint alone.** **Second, and more useful, an outside source dated February 2026 draws the distinction this page keeps having to re-draw under headline pressure: the $500bn STARGATE JV was stalled AT THE JV LEVEL while the BILATERAL contracts — the $300bn / 4.5GW OpenAI deal and the Abilene groundbreaking — were moving. Those are separate objects, and a JV headline is not a backlog datapoint.** ⚠️⚠️ **The campus-scale figures that arrive with that framing (1.2GW Abilene, a 10GW phase, a 2.8GW power agreement) are PRESS-REPORTED and are explicitly barred from this page’s facility-GW ledger; they are logged as claims, not as capacity.** **On the margin side, Citi’s June channel work — multiyear hyperscaler and neocloud renewals repricing “as much as 2x higher” — is the first third-party mechanism on this page by which OCI gross margin could recover without a mix change, and it sits directly against the standing Redburn bear case. No margin estimate was moved on it.**

**Quarter synthesis — ADDENDUM 2026-09-02 (1 source: Jefferies' 58pp "AI Deep Dive" chart deck, Brent Thill — the hyperscaler/software companion to the same day's ORCL deep dive).** **NOTHING SUPERSEDED AND NO RATING OR PT MOVED — BUY / $290 is a reiteration of the mark cut into this page earlier the same day, and the four overhangs are restated unchanged.** The synthesis above said the debate has migrated from demand to financing; this source leaves that intact and sharpens ONE of its two components. **The counterparty-concentration leg stops being a percentage and becomes an arithmetic: $300bn of announced frontier-lab deals against $338bn of RPO ex-frontier, footing to the disclosed $638bn.** More usefully, it puts that 47% in a peer frame for the first time — [[AMZN]] 48%, GOOGL 38%, MSFT 37% — which reclassifies the bear from *"Oracle is unusually exposed to the AI labs"* (it is not; Amazon is more so) to *"Oracle's exposure is unusually UNDIVERSIFIED"* (one counterparty vs several). ➤ **That is a narrower and more defensible bear than the one the tape has been trading, and it is the version this page should carry into the F1Q27 print, scored against the bogey grid already logged (115% OCI growth, 41% opm, 40% RPO growth).** ⚠️ The financing leg is untouched by this source: negative FCF to FY30E, ~4.5x net leverage and ~$260bn of off-balance-sheet DC commitments all stand exactly as logged.

**Quarter synthesis — ADDENDUM 2026-09-06 (1 source: Deutsche Bank · Brad Zelnick, "Oracle — F1Q Preview: Fundamentals in-tact, execution in-focus", 12pp; Buy, PT $300 reiterated — DCF-derived, 34x CY27E EPS; ref $154.04, ~−19% YTD).** **THE QUARTER'S DEBATE HAS NOW FULLY COLLAPSED ONTO ONE DISCLOSURE.** Four houses independently converge on the same F1Q OCI bogey (~115% y/y; DB/Street, Jefferies 115%, BofA IaaS +116%) and the same physical enabler (~1GW of deliveries, vs 400MW+ in each of the prior two quarters) — so **the topline is no longer where houses disagree; the disagreement is entirely about whether the capacity lands, at what margin, and how it is paid for.** DB's contribution is to split the margin question in two — a **61% non-GAAP GROSS margin (−7+pts y/y, persisting all year)** against a nearly-flat **−1pt non-GAAP OPERATING margin** and **NGOI accelerating to the mid-20s from +16%** — which means the page's existing 41% / 40.9% operating-margin bogeys do NOT test the gross-margin call, and a print can beat one line while missing the other. On funding, DB supplies the missing anchor (**December's <$100bn ringfence of incremental capital needs**), the running tally (**net ~$28.5bn raised via debt + convertible preferred**), the forward intention (**$40bn FY27 debt+equity incl. the $20bn ATM**), and the timing (**an official ATM progress update expected AT the print**) — while arguing financing-mix downside alone is limited here. **The ATM sign conflict is now THREE-SIDED and stays open by design: credit wants the equity leg, equity calls it dilution, DB calls the mix near-neutral.** ⚠️ **Untouched by this source and unchanged: the OpenAI/RPO concentration split (UBS ~40% / Jefferies ~47% / JPM ~50%+), the leverage and spread bears, and the New Mexico / Project Jupiter delivery item.**

**🆕 Quarter synthesis — WINDOW CLOSED 2026-09-10 BY THE FQ1 FY27 PRINT (Oracle earnings call, BBG LIVE transcript, 2026-09-10).** **HOW THE QUARTER'S ONE DEBATE ACTUALLY RESOLVED: the window collapsed onto FINANCING, and financing was answered — but only for the near term.** The **$20bn ATM was completed IN ENTIRETY in Q1**, which is the cleanest possible resolution of the three-sided sign conflict this page refused to pre-resolve (JPM IG credit wanted the equity leg; the equity side called it dilution overhang; DB expected only a progress update). **FY27 capex was HELD at $90-95bn / net cash ≤$70bn**, so the "capex guide is a plateau that cannot hold" bear did not get its catalyst either. Against that, **the multi-year gap is untouched**: no FCF-positive date ("we don't expect to give that today"), Q1 FCF −$5bn, and Clay's own correction — *"it doesn't require additional CASH from Oracle,"* not additional capex — pushes the prepay/BYOH mechanic closer to MS Credit's economically-debt reading than to the bulls'. **On the DEMAND side the window's bogeys were beaten and the CAPACITY bogey was missed: OCI +121% vs the +115-120% cluster, but 850MW delivered vs the ~1GW that DB, UBS, JPM and Barclays all modelled** — a distinction worth keeping because the capacity number is the physical constraint and the revenue number is the outcome. **The genuinely NEW axis this print opens is RESIDUAL VALUE: GPUs four years or older renewed or resold at a 20% PREMIUM, at 97.9% utilisation.** No source in this whole window had a price-based rebuttal to the depreciation-schedule bear; management now does, on an undisclosed cohort. **And the one thing the window's bear case was built on that survived intact is NEW MEXICO: FY27 was ring-fenced, FY28 was not mentioned, the air permit is still outstanding and the 09-01 court stay went unaddressed.** ⚠️ **Source is a LIVE transcript that warns it may not be accurate; re-ingest the FINAL transcript.**

Gross capex was $28.5bn against JPMe of $20.0bn (a 43% overshoot in one quarter) yet FCF at −$5.4bn still beat consensus −$10.0bn, because the prepayments carried it.** 🔴 **ON THE $30bn OF NEW AI CONTRACTS: they *"still require capex but NOT incremental cash from Oracle"*, funded by supplier financing, customer-purchased hardware or prepayments, and — the timing detail that matters — they *"will NOT AFFECT REVENUE OR CAPEX UNTIL FY28 OR BEYOND"*.** 🔴 **MANAGEMENT DID NOT PUSH BACK on FY27/FY28 being the peak capex years, and while it reiterated the FY27 GM step-down it indicated *"FLATTENING IS REASONABLE TO EXPECT AS THE RAMP COMPLETES"*.** ⚠️⚠️ **NO TIMELINE WAS GIVEN FOR POSITIVE FREE CASH FLOW** — the unit economics claim instead is that *"each project is expected to convert ~100% OF POST-TAX EBITDA TO FREE CASH FLOW ONCE RAMPED."* 🔴 **AND THE ANSWER TO THIS WIKI'S STANDING MEMORY-COST QUESTION, FOR ORACLE: *"management stated COMPONENT PRICE INCREASES ARE BEING PASSED THROUGH without changing the long-term AI infrastructure gross margin framework"* — the opposite of what [[AAPL]] did with the same input cost in the same week.** Capacity: the 850MW landed into a fleet at **97.9% GPU utilisation** and included **none of Shackelford, New Mexico, Wisconsin or Michigan**; Abilene alone **131,000 GPUs (1.9x F4Q), now 75% handed over**. ⚠️ **New Mexico is on track *"pending an AIR PERMIT FOR ON-SITE [[BE]] BLOOM FUEL CELLS"* — the permit this page has tracked now has a named cause.** |

No number adopted; "may now be higher" is a direction, not a mark.** ⚠️ **THE CAPACITY MISS UBS OWNS UP TO: *"the 850 MW of capacity added FELL SHORT OF THE ~1 GW TARGET"* — softened as *"just short of what Microsoft added last quarter"* and a large ramp regardless.** Other positives: opex −9% *"tightly managed to offset GM pressure"*; **dollar capex reaffirmed to peak in FY27 or FY28**; ATM completion means **no additional financing required this calendar year**; SaaS +10% *"accelerated by a point sequentially"*. ⚠️ **Negatives named: Oracle MERELY REAFFIRMED the $90bn FY27 revenue guide set back on the 2Q/Nov-2025 call despite stronger capacity, backlog and GPU pricing — citing conservatism, "this is 1Q", and capacity timing/conversion uncertainty; the 2Q guide range is *"very wide… signaling some source of uncertainty"*; and on-prem licence *"was down hard in the quarter"* (JPM: Software −3% y/y; DB: licence 9% below Street).** Valuation: 23x CY27 GAAP / 18x non-GAAP. |

| **09-18** | 🆕 **Morgan Stanley · Sanjit K Singh / Adam Wood / Jamie Reynolds — "Databricks: First Data, Today AI, Next the Agentic Enterprise" (Foundation, 40pp)** ⚠️ **READ-THROUGH ONLY: the subject is DATABRICKS, a PRIVATE company with no page in this wiki. MS states no investment recommendation is provided and the content is unaudited; Oracle is named three times in the note and no Oracle estimate, rating or PT is offered** | **competicao / produto** | **bear (intent) · neutral (traction)** | 🔴 **THE NAMED OVERLAP IS OLTP, NOT THE WAREHOUSE — AND ORACLE IS NAMED: "With Lakebase, Databricks looks to expand from the analytical data market into the transactional database market, bringing it into competition with a newer set of competitors, including ORACLE and MongoDB (among others)."** Repeated in the category framing: Databricks competes *"increasingly [with] transactional databases such as ORACLE, SQL Server, Amazon Aurora and MongoDB."* **Lakebase is a managed, serverless PostgreSQL service** (compute separated from durable storage, autoscaling, scale-to-zero, branching) built from the **Neon acquisition (~$1bn, May 2025)**, **GA on AWS January 2026 and on Azure March 2026, GCP still in beta**, crossing a **>$100m revenue run-rate**; **LTAP** is the companion play to run transactional and analytical workloads on one store and kill the CDC/ETL hop between them. ⚠️ **WHAT THE NOTE DOES *NOT* SAY, AND IT IS THE PART THAT CUTS ORACLE'S WAY — MS's own risk section leads with the convergence failing: "Success in penetrating the transactional database market with LakeBase and converging the OLTP and OLAP through LTAP is NOT A GUARANTEE… the unification of OLTP and OLAP has been attempted before, LARGELY UNSUCCESSFULLY. As a result, customers may opt to RETAIN EXISTING TRANSACTIONAL SYSTEMS even while expanding their use of Databricks for analytics."** And the displacement bar is set high on purpose: *"Replacing an established system of record requires a separate assessment of application compatibility, availability requirements and migration effort"*; the immediate risk across the whole landscape is *"SLOWER EXPANSION as customers select different engines for different workloads, rather than wholesale replacement."* **Also: Databricks does not currently offer one permission system governing both PostgreSQL writes and lakehouse analytics** — a stated product gap in the thing attacking Oracle's estate. ➤ **How this lands against what the page already carries: the standing bear is that greenfield data/AI-platform projects "often favor competitors such as Databricks, [[SNOW]], Postgres or [[MDB]]" (09-10/09-11 window). This SPECIFIES the mechanism for the DATABASE half of Oracle rather than the OCI half — a $7bn-run-rate, >80%-growth private platform with 20,000+ customers (>70% of the F500) now pointing a cost-led serverless Postgres at the system of record — while simultaneously supplying the incumbent's defence (no proof the convergence works; migration friction; a governance gap).** ⚠️ **NO LAKEBASE CUSTOMER COUNT, NO ORACLE-DISPLACEMENT EXAMPLE AND NO SHARE NUMBER ANYWHERE IN THE NOTE — intent and a run-rate, not traction against Oracle.** ⚠️ **Do not read the $100m Lakebase run-rate or the ~$540bn 2030 Data+AI TAM (IDC/MS) against any Oracle line on this page — different bases.** [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/DATABRICKS_20260918_0401.html) |

That is a testable claim about mix-vs-price, not a vague "moving up".** 🔴 **AND THE PRICE INFLATION QUANTIFIED, FIRST TIME ON THIS PAGE FROM THIS SOURCE: *"Oracle disclosed that **GPU pricing is up on average 20% on renewals and resales of capacity**"* — put alongside NVDA's *"the price of renting an H100 has risen 20% year-to-date, while A100 cloud pricing is up nearly 15%"*, IREN's *"3-year contract pricing is up 125% since November, and 5-year contract pricing is up 70%"*, and OpenAI's CFO on a podcast: *"the compute I bought a year ago could sell in the market today for 3-5x"* — to which UBS adds the barb that this *"might also suggest that the terms offered to OpenAI from the likes of Microsoft, Oracle, and CoreWeave could have been very generous."*** 🔴 **CUSTOMER-FUNDED CAPEX, WITH THE COHORT AROUND IT: *"Oracle said that of its expected **FY27 capex of $90-95 billion, $20-25 billion will be funded by customers** via either pre-pays or **'bring your own hardware'** deals"* (≈22-26%) — against **[[NBIS]] at 50-60%** self-funded and **IREN at 45-55%**, with [[CRWV]] prepays at 15-25% of TCV. ➤ **Oracle is the LEAST customer-funded of the four on UBS's own numbers, which is the funding-strain leg of this page's debate expressed as a ratio rather than a narrative.** UBS separately notes *"Oracle has cited efforts of customers (**we believe OpenAI**) to 'bring your own hardware'"* — the BYOH counterparty is now named, as a belief.** 🔴 **A PER-RACK REVENUE MARK FROM THE CO-CEO, NET-NEW TO THIS PAGE: *"Oracle's co-CEO gave an estimate of **$8 million in revenue per Vera Rubin rack**."*** ⚠️ **BASIS: annual contract revenue per NVL72-class rack. Goldman's bottom-up now supplies one: 2.45 GW — i.e. ~56% of the ~4.35 GW of announced US fuel-cell projects it can identify sits on a single Oracle site. That makes the OCTOBER 19, 2026 air-quality hearing already diarised on this page not just an Oracle catalyst but the single largest swing factor in the US fuel-cell forecast.** ⚠️ **BASIS: this is INSTALLED FUEL-CELL NAMEPLATE for the site, from GS's own bottom-up of company announcements — it is not IT load, not contracted compute, and not the 1.5 GW "Doña Ana NM" figure this page carries from the Stargate pipeline. Generation nameplate is overbuilt relative to IT capacity by design; GS models fuel cells as the LEAST overbuilt technology at ~+9% (725 MW installed for 665 MW required on a 500 MW IT-capacity data centre) against RICE +22%, OCGT +26% and CCGT +47%. Do not net 2,450 MW of fuel cells against any Oracle IT-MW or compute-GW figure.** 🔴 **AND IT CORROBORATES THE COST NUMBER THIS PAGE ALREADY CARRIES FROM REDBURN (p43: fuel cells at *"roughly $0.11-0.13/kWh"*): GS's independent LCOE build puts fuel cells at **$117/MWh** — the most expensive of four options, *"roughly 45% higher than CCGT and c.30% higher than OCGT"* — falling to **$90/MWh with the US 30% ITC**, against reciprocating engines **$80/MWh** and CCGT **$81/MWh** at $4/mmbtu gas. Installed cost **$4,750/kW** for fuel cells (to ~$2,700/kW after the §48E ITC, which runs through 2035 with no phase-out) vs $1,800 RICE / $2,400 OCGT / $2,600 CCGT. ➤ **Two houses, independent methods, same answer: Oracle's New Mexico power solution is the costliest per MWh on the board. GS's counter is that the choice is not being made on LCOE — *"technology selection is increasingly driven by execution-related considerations such as speed-to-power, reliability and permitting certainty"*, on which fuel cells score best (76.6/100 vs 68.2 aeroderivative GT, 67.0 RICE, 59.6 heavy-duty GT/CCGT), with **6-12 month deployment** against **5-7 years** for heavy-duty gas turbines. Which is exactly the trade Oracle made when it pivoted off the gas pipeline in April 2026 — and it now has a third-party cost of that decision.** |

**③ THE ACCOUNTING MECHANISM BEHIND THE CALL, NOW WITH THE FORMULA AND THE SIZING (pp49-51).** The relay carried *"lease-cost inflation, huge lease commitments for assets that have not yet commenced, declining ROI"*. The primary supplies what sits underneath it: *"Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle combined have more than $1trn of lease commitments for assets that have not yet commenced"*; at *"an average YoC of c19%"* those commitments represent *"roughly $300-400bn of self-build-equivalent infrastructure capital"*; and **economic free cash flow is defined explicitly as reported FCF less the change in implied capital, where implied capital = uncommenced lease commitments ÷ lease duration ÷ yield on cost.**

- 🔴 **AND ORACLE'S RANK IS THIRD, NOT FIRST — WHICH THE RELAY DID NOT SAY.** *"Microsoft screens as the most exposed, with approximately $330bn of uncommenced lease commitments, followed by Meta and Oracle."* The same ordering holds on lease-cost inflation exposure: *"[Microsoft has the] greatest exposure to this lease-cost inflation, followed by Meta and Oracle."* ➤ **This materially softens how the 21h block reads. Oracle is named in a Sell, but on Redburn's own exposure ranking it sits behind two Neutral-rated names — so the uncommenced-lease exposure cannot by itself be what separates the ORCL Sell from the MSFT Neutral. What separates them, on the numbers in ②, is the out-year EPS gap.**

**🆕 Quarter synthesis — Q2 FY27 window, as at 2026-09-21 (RE-EVALUATED on the Redburn primary; nothing superseded).** The window opened on the FQ1 FY27 print having answered financing for the near term — the **$20bn ATM was completed in its entirety in Q1** — and having left, in GS · Borges's words, *"the LARGER DEBATE… FY28 AND BEYOND"*. If Oracle re-commits to an FY30 ladder consistent with consensus margins, Redburn is wrong in a way that will be visible within one meeting; if it guides FY28 capital intensity higher without a matching margin bridge, the (14.6%) out-year operating-profit gap becomes the largest live divergence on this page.** ⚠️ **Two items are explicitly NOT resolved by this source and stay open: the Project Jupiter SPV structure — Redburn corroborates a $300bn CONTRACT value for the July-2025 OpenAI agreement and says nothing whatever about a vehicle, four sites, 1,400 acres or Blue Owl — and the New Mexico loans quoted at 89-91 cents, which the 148-page note does not mention at all. | Margins / profitability | Op margin 43% | Op margin 43%, +60bps | Op margin 44% | AI-capacity gross margin 32% (vs 30%+ guide) | GM down ~5pts FY26; ROIC "high 20s" | **Non-GAAP op income $8.2bn +31%; non-GAAP OPERATING MARGIN ~flat at 42%**; **gross margin declined "as expected"** on data-centre ramp + infra-revenue mix, offset by lower opex and "simplification and efficiency actions" — **NO gross-margin number and NO gross-margin guide given**; CFO expects GM to "flatten" after the ramp, detail deferred to the **October Investor Day**; no component-cost impact expected on GM, with pass-through pricing |

The reason it matters beyond this page: if local opposition is being underwritten by lenders rather than absorbed by developers, the constraint migrates from PERMITTING (a schedule problem) to COST OF CAPITAL (a returns problem) — and the second is the one that actually caps capex.   🔴 **DB's FY28 base case is net-new to this page: "another ~$40BN OF INCREMENTAL CAPITAL WOULD BE REQUIRED IN FY28, based on forecasted GROSS CAPEX OF ~$120BN and a similar level of customer prepayments as this year ($20-$25BN)" — with DB flagging that as "PROBABLY TOWARD THE HIGH END of possible capital needs." Also added, as marks rather than views: UBS's peer table at 7/24/26 has **ORCL $114.99, -41% YTD, $335,196m market cap, revenue growth +20.6% MRQ / +25.9% CY26E / +38.7% CY27E — the fastest CY27E grower in the twelve-name comp set — at 15.4x/12.6x CY26E/CY27E non-GAAP P/E vs a peer-group mean of 16.1x/14.4x** (MSFT 19.2x/16.9x). The dispersion itself is the signal on this name.** **Two independent negatives landed the same day and both bear on the funding question: (1) the IG bond market is showing 'digestion issues' — [[NVDA]], [[SPCX]] and [[AMZN]] each sold $25bn within weeks, widening spreads 20-40bps — which is precisely the market Oracle must access while protecting the rating its CFO was discussing; (2) [[OPENAI]] disintermediation moved from theory to a named site: Project Camellia, its $20bn Effingham County, GA datacentre, 'represents the FIRST SITE IN WHICH OPENAI IS THE LEAD DESIGNER AND DEVELOPER. At its other sites, OpenAI RENTS CHIPS FROM CLOUD PROVIDERS SUCH AS ORACLE AND AMAZON WEB SERVICES.'** Read together with the CFO's pacing language, the risk is that Oracle slows its build for balance-sheet reasons exactly as its anchor customer learns to build for itself. Tape: **ORCL sits on Rothschild's 12-month relative-lows list** (with [[TSLA]], APP, SNAP, [[UBER]], CHTR, PSKY). Cross-filed [[OPENAI]], [[AMZN]], [[MSFT]], [[NVDA]], [themes/hyperscaler-capex](themes/hyperscaler-capex.md).
